Table 5
Reducibility, Undecidability, and Computation Histories
1. Q: What is reducibility, and why is it the central method introduced in this chapter for proving undecidability?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Reducibility converts instances of one problem into instances of another so that a solver for the second would solve the first.

Reducibility is a way to compare computational problems by showing how a solution procedure for one problem can be used as a component in solving another. If problem A reduces to problem B, an instance of A is transformed or otherwise handled so that an assumed solver for B supplies the information needed to answer A. The method does not require solving A or B independently; it establishes a dependency between them. For undecidability proofs, begin with a problem already known to be undecidable, commonly ATM, and reduce it to the new target B. If B had a decider, the reduction would yield a decider for ATM, contradicting its established undecidability. The contradiction proves that B is undecidable.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the direction of a reduction in your own words and reconstruct the implication from a hypothetical solver for B to a solver for A."

Key point:
- A reduction transfers the ability to solve B into a method for solving A.
- To prove B undecidable, reduce a known undecidable problem to B.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
2. Q: What does the statement that A reduces to B mean about the relative difficulty of the two problems?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A is no harder than B under the particular reduction being used.

The statement A reduces to B means that a method for solving B can be used, together with the reduction procedure, to solve A. Informally, B is at least as difficult as A with respect to that reduction. This is a one-way comparison, not a claim that the problems are equivalent or that B reduces to A. It also does not assert that A or B is easy, nor does it measure practical running time unless the reduction is explicitly constrained by a complexity measure. In computability arguments, the important consequence is logical: if B is decidable, then A is decidable. Therefore, if A is known to be undecidable and A reduces to B, B cannot be decidable.

Study instruction:
- "Draw an arrow A → B and annotate it with the sentence “a solver for B gives a solver for A.” Use that sentence to check every later proof."

Key point:
- The reduction arrow points from the problem being solved indirectly to the problem whose solver is assumed.
- The implication is one-way; do not reverse it without a separate proof.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
3. Q: How is an undecidability proof by reduction normally structured?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Assume the target problem has a decider, then use it to construct a decider for a problem already known to be undecidable.

First choose a suitable source language A whose undecidability is established. Let B be the target language. Assume for contradiction that a Turing machine R decides B. Then describe an effective construction that uses R to decide A: on input x, transform x or construct a machine encoding derived from x, run R on the transformed instance, and interpret R's answer so that the result is correct for membership in A. Prove both directions of correctness and show that the constructed machine halts on every input, relying on the fact that the reduction itself is effective and R is assumed to halt. This would make A decidable, contradicting the known theorem. The assumption that R exists must therefore be false.

Study instruction:
- "Write a proof skeleton with four parts: known undecidable source, assumed target decider, constructed source decider, and contradiction. Fill in correctness before writing prose."

Key point:
- A reduction proof must specify a realizable construction, not merely claim that two problems are related.
- The contradiction depends on a correct decider for the source problem.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
4. Q: Why is it not enough to simulate a Turing machine directly when trying to decide whether it accepts an input?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A direct simulation may run forever when the simulated machine loops, so it cannot serve as a decider.

To decide ATM, a machine must accept when the encoded machine accepts the input and reject when it rejects or runs forever. A direct simulation correctly detects acceptance and rejection if either occurs, but it cannot recognize in finite time that an arbitrary computation will never halt. If the simulated machine loops, the proposed procedure also loops, violating the definition of a decider. Reducibility avoids this obstacle by assuming a decider for another problem that supplies information unavailable from the direct simulation. For example, a hypothetical decider for HALTTM can first determine whether the simulation will halt; only then is it safe to simulate until the halting outcome appears.

Study instruction:
- "Distinguish “the machine has not halted yet” from “the machine will never halt.” Explain why no finite simulation prefix alone establishes the latter."

Key point:
- A decider must halt on every input, including negative instances.
- An unfinished simulation is not evidence that a computation will run forever.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
5. Q: How does reducing ATM to HALTTM prove that the halting problem is undecidable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A hypothetical HALTTM decider would let us decide whether a machine accepts a given input.

Assume R decides HALTTM, where HALTTM contains encodings ⟨M,w⟩ for which M eventually halts on w, whether by accepting or rejecting. Construct S for ATM. On input ⟨M,w⟩, run R on the same pair. If R rejects, M does not halt, so S rejects because M cannot accept. If R accepts, simulate M on w until it halts; this simulation is guaranteed to terminate by R's answer. Accept if M accepts and reject if M rejects. Thus S halts on every input and decides ATM. Since ATM is undecidable, the assumed R cannot exist, so HALTTM is undecidable.

Study instruction:
- "Trace all three cases—accept, reject, and loop—and record which branch of S handles each one."

Key point:
- HALTTM distinguishes halting from nonhalting, not acceptance from rejection.
- The preliminary halting test makes the subsequent simulation safe.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
6. Q: What is the emptiness problem ETM, and why is it undecidable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: ETM asks whether a Turing machine recognizes no strings; its undecidability follows by encoding a particular ATM instance into a modified machine.

ETM = {⟨M⟩ | M is a Turing machine and L(M) = ∅}. To reduce ATM to ETM, take an input ⟨M,w⟩ and construct M1 that rejects every input x other than w. On input w, M1 simulates M on w and accepts exactly if M accepts. Consequently, L(M1) is nonempty exactly when M accepts w; it is empty exactly when M does not accept w. If a decider R for ETM existed, a machine could construct M1, run R on ⟨M1⟩, and reverse the answer to decide ATM. This contradiction proves ETM undecidable. The construction must encode w into M1's finite description and compare its actual input with w.

Study instruction:
- "Re-derive the equivalence L(M1) = ∅ iff M does not accept w, then identify why the answer from the emptiness decider is reversed."

Key point:
- The modified machine can accept only the designated string w.
- A language is nonempty if at least one input is accepted; it need not contain w unless the construction enforces that.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
7. Q: Why does a reduction to ETM need to modify the source machine instead of simply testing whether L(M) is empty?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Nonemptiness of L(M) only says that M accepts some string, not necessarily the particular input w in the ATM instance.

Given ⟨M,w⟩, a direct test of whether L(M) is empty does not answer whether M accepts w. If L(M) is nonempty, the accepted string could be unrelated to w. The reduction therefore constructs M1 so that all inputs except w are rejected immediately, while w triggers the original computation of M on w. This restriction makes the existential statement “M1 accepts some input” equivalent to the specific statement “M accepts w.” The key design principle is to control the set of possible witnesses so that the target problem's broad property becomes equivalent to the exact source question.

Study instruction:
- "For a proposed reduction, list all inputs the constructed machine might accept. Check that each possible witness corresponds to the source instance you intend to test."

Key point:
- A reduction must preserve the truth of the particular membership question.
- Restricting the constructed language can turn an existential property into a test of one chosen input.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
8. Q: How is REGULARTM shown to be undecidable using a language that is regular or nonregular depending on acceptance?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Construct a machine whose language is Σ if M accepts w and {0ⁿ1ⁿ | n ≥ 0} otherwise.

REGULARTM = {⟨M⟩ | L(M) is regular}. For an ATM instance ⟨M,w⟩, construct M2 that accepts every string of the form 0ⁿ1ⁿ. On any other input, it simulates M on the fixed input w and accepts if that simulation accepts. If M accepts w, M2 accepts every string, so L(M2) = Σ, which is regular. If M does not accept w, the second branch never accepts and L(M2) = {0ⁿ1ⁿ | n ≥ 0}, a known nonregular language. A decider for REGULARTM would therefore decide ATM by testing ⟨M2⟩. The constructed M2 is fed to the hypothetical decider; it need not be run to completion on an arbitrary input during the reduction.

Study instruction:
- "Explain why the two possible languages of M2 are exactly Σ* and the nonregular base language, and why the base language is nonregular."

Key point:
- The target property must change exactly when the source instance changes from no to yes.
- A reduction often constructs a machine description rather than executing the constructed machine.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
9. Q: How does the equivalence problem EQTM inherit undecidability from ETM?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Compare an arbitrary machine with a fixed machine that recognizes the empty language.

EQTM = {⟨M1,M2⟩ | L(M1) = L(M2)}. Let M∅ be a fixed Turing machine that rejects every input, so L(M∅) = ∅. Map an ETM instance ⟨M⟩ to ⟨M,M∅⟩. The two languages are equal exactly when L(M) is empty. Thus ETM ≤m EQTM. If EQTM had a decider, it would decide ETM by comparing M with M∅. Because ETM is undecidable, EQTM is undecidable. This is a useful pattern: a general comparison problem can encode a one-object property by fixing the second object to a carefully selected constant.

Study instruction:
- "Write the reduction function f(⟨M⟩) = ⟨M,M∅⟩ and prove the membership equivalence in both directions."

Key point:
- A fixed reference object can turn an equivalence test into an emptiness test.
- The reduction preserves equality of languages, not equality of machine descriptions.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
10. Q: What is a computation history of a Turing machine?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: It is the finite sequence of configurations that records a complete halting computation on a particular input.

A configuration is a snapshot of a Turing machine at one instant: it records the current state, the tape contents relevant to the computation, and the head position. A computation history is a sequence C1,C2,…,Cl in which C1 is the start configuration, every later configuration follows legally from its predecessor according to the transition function, and the final configuration is halting. An accepting computation history ends in an accept state; a rejecting one ends in a reject state. Such histories are finite. A machine that runs forever on the input has no accepting or rejecting computation history for that run. The sequence makes a potentially long computation available as a finite object whose local transition consistency can be checked.

Study instruction:
- "Construct a short sample history for a simple machine and annotate the state, head position, and tape contents in every configuration."

Key point:
- A history is a complete record of a particular computation, not a description of all possible runs.
- Each adjacent pair must satisfy the machine's transition rule.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
11. Q: How do accepting and rejecting computation histories differ?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: They have the same start and transition-validity requirements but end in different halting states.

Both types begin with the designated start configuration for M on w and require every configuration to follow legally from the previous one. An accepting history ends in a configuration containing the accept state; a rejecting history ends in a configuration containing the reject state. The distinction matters in reductions because many target properties are designed to recognize only accepting histories. If M accepts w, at least one accepting history exists; if it does not accept, none exists, even if it may reject or loop. A history cannot be accepted as valid merely because its last configuration looks accepting: its initial configuration and every intervening transition must also be correct.

Study instruction:
- "Test a candidate history against three separate checks: correct start, legal transitions, and correct final state."

Key point:
- A valid final state does not compensate for an invalid earlier transition.
- Acceptance is represented by existence of a complete valid accepting history.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
12. Q: Why are computation histories useful in undecidability reductions?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: They turn a machine's acceptance behavior into a finite string whose structural validity can be checked by another computational model.

A reduction can construct a recognizer for encodings of accepting computation histories of M on w. If M accepts w, such a history exists and the constructed language is nonempty. If M does not accept w, no such history exists and the language is empty. This allows an acceptance question about an unrestricted Turing machine to be encoded as a language property of a more restricted model, such as an LBA. Computation histories are especially useful when the target problem asks whether some object exists, because the history itself serves as a witness. The reduction still must show that the constructed verifier is implementable and checks the full history rather than merely a plausible fragment.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the witness in an existence problem and specify how a verifier checks its initial condition, local transitions, and accepting endpoint."

Key point:
- The history is the certificate; the reduction controls which certificates are valid.
- Existence of a valid accepting history is equivalent to acceptance by the original machine.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
13. Q: What is a linear bounded automaton, and how does its tape restriction affect its computational power?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: An LBA is a Turing machine whose head is confined to the input portion of the tape, giving it only linearly bounded space.

A linear bounded automaton is a restricted Turing machine that cannot move its tape head beyond the portion of tape occupied by the input. Attempts to cross either boundary leave the head at the boundary. For an input of length n, the machine has n tape cells, each holding one of g tape symbols, and can use a larger tape alphabet to obtain a constant-factor increase in representable information. Its available storage therefore grows linearly with input length. LBAs remain powerful: the chapter notes that they can decide several automata and grammar problems, including membership for context-free languages. The space restriction creates a finite configuration space for each fixed input, which is the key to deciding ALBA.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an unrestricted Turing machine with an LBA by listing which tape positions each may access on an input of length n."

Key point:
- An LBA has input-bounded work space rather than an unbounded tape.
- The finite number of configurations on a fixed input enables a halting bound.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
14. Q: How many configurations can an LBA have on an input of length n?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: With q states and g tape symbols, the configuration count is q · n · gⁿ.

A configuration is determined by three independent choices. The control can be in one of q states. The head can occupy one of n input-tape positions. The tape can contain any of gⁿ possible strings of length n over the tape alphabet. Multiplying these choices gives qngⁿ configurations. This is an upper bound on the number of distinct snapshots available during one computation on that input. The count is finite even though it can be exponentially large in n. If a deterministic LBA continues for more steps than the number of possible configurations without halting, some configuration must repeat, by the pigeonhole principle.

Study instruction:
- "Derive qngⁿ from the three components of a configuration and calculate it for a small hypothetical q, n, and g."

Key point:
- The state, head position, and tape contents jointly determine a configuration.
- Finiteness, not smallness, is what matters for decidability.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
15. Q: Why is ALBA decidable even though ATM is undecidable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: An LBA has only finitely many configurations on a fixed input, so repeated configurations reveal looping.

ALBA asks whether a given LBA accepts a given input. Simulate the LBA while counting steps, stopping if it accepts or rejects. For an LBA with q states, g tape symbols, and input length n, there are qngⁿ configurations. A deterministic computation that has not halted after this many steps must have repeated a configuration. Because the transition function is deterministic, the future behavior from the repeated configuration repeats as well, so the machine will loop and cannot later accept. A decider can therefore reject after the bound. This contrasts with unrestricted Turing machines, which may use arbitrarily much tape and have no comparable finite configuration bound for a fixed input.

Study instruction:
- "Prove the loop-detection argument using the pigeonhole principle, and explain why determinism makes a repeated configuration conclusive."

Key point:
- The configuration bound supplies a guaranteed stopping rule.
- The ALBA result does not imply that every problem about LBA languages is decidable.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
16. Q: What is the algorithm for deciding ALBA, and why does it always halt?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Simulate the LBA for at most qngⁿ steps, accepting or rejecting according to its halting state and rejecting if the bound is exceeded.

On input ⟨M,w⟩, verify that the encoding describes an LBA and determine its state count q, tape alphabet size g, and input length n. Simulate M on w for at most qngⁿ steps. If M accepts within the bound, accept; if it rejects, reject. If it has not halted by the end of the bound, reject because a configuration has repeated and deterministic behavior will cycle. The algorithm halts because the step limit is a computable finite integer derived from the input description. The bound may be impractically large, but decidability concerns guaranteed termination, not efficient running time.

Study instruction:
- "Write pseudocode for the bounded simulation and identify the exact point at which the algorithm rejects a looping computation."

Key point:
- A computable finite bound is enough to establish decidability.
- A decidable algorithm may have a very large running-time bound.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
17. Q: Why can the emptiness problem ELBA still be undecidable when ALBA is decidable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Deciding one LBA on one fixed input is different from deciding whether its language contains any accepted input.

ALBA asks a membership question about a specific pair ⟨M,w⟩. ELBA asks whether there exists any string accepted by M. The input to ELBA is only a machine description, and the possible witnesses range over all strings. Although each individual LBA computation on a fixed string can be decided, there is no finite input-length bound that lets us exhaustively test all strings. The chapter proves ELBA undecidable by reducing ATM to it: build an LBA whose language consists precisely of accepting computation histories of the original machine on its designated input. The LBA's language is empty exactly when the original machine does not accept.

Study instruction:
- "State the quantifier in each problem: ALBA concerns one specified input, while ELBA concerns whether there exists any accepted input."

Key point:
- Decidability of membership does not automatically decide emptiness over an infinite domain.
- The reduction encodes acceptance as existence of a valid history.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
18. Q: How is the LBA used in the reduction from ATM to ELBA?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Construct an LBA B that accepts exactly the accepting computation histories of M on w.

Given ⟨M,w⟩, build B to accept an input string x only if x encodes an accepting computation history for M on w. B checks that the first configuration is the start configuration, that each adjacent pair of configurations obeys M's transition function, and that the last configuration is accepting. If M accepts w, its finite accepting computation supplies at least one string accepted by B, so L(B) is nonempty. If M does not accept w, no valid accepting history exists and L(B) is empty. A hypothetical ELBA decider could test B's language and, with its answer interpreted appropriately, decide ATM. Thus ELBA is undecidable.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how B checks the first, middle, and final parts of a candidate history, and prove the nonempty-language equivalence."

Key point:
- B recognizes histories, not arbitrary simulations on its own unbounded tape.
- The existence of one accepted history captures the original acceptance event.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
19. Q: Which checks must an LBA verifier perform to validate an accepting computation history?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: It must verify the start configuration, every legal one-step transition, and an accepting final configuration.

For a candidate string encoded as configurations separated by delimiters, B first parses the sequence and checks that its first configuration is exactly the prescribed start configuration q0w (with the appropriate tape representation). It then examines each consecutive pair Ci,Ci+1 and verifies that all unchanged tape cells agree and that the state, head position, and locally affected tape symbols reflect one legal transition of M. Finally, it checks that the last configuration contains the accept state. A candidate that omits a step, changes an unrelated cell, uses an illegal transition, or ends in a nonaccepting state must be rejected. Delimiters and markers allow a finite-control LBA to navigate and compare corresponding positions using only input-bounded space.

Study instruction:
- "Create an invalid history with one deliberately corrupted transition and identify which local comparison detects the error."

Key point:
- Local transition checks establish global computational validity when applied to every adjacent pair.
- The accepting endpoint and correct start are separate conditions.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
20. Q: How does the chapter reduce ATM to ALLCFG?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: It constructs a grammar that generates every string except encodings of valid accepting computation histories.

ALLCFG = {⟨G⟩ | L(G) = Σ}. Given M and w, the construction makes a context-free grammar G generate strings that are not valid accepting computation histories of M on w. Such a string can fail because it has the wrong initial configuration, does not end in an accepting configuration, or contains an illegal adjacent transition. If M does not accept w, no valid accepting history exists, so every string is generated and L(G)=Σ. If M accepts w, at least one valid history is omitted, so L(G) is not Σ*. A decider for ALLCFG would decide ATM by interpreting the answer in the opposite way, proving ALLCFG undecidable.

Study instruction:
- "Explain why the grammar generates invalid histories rather than valid ones, and show how that choice determines the answer reversal."

Key point:
- The complement-of-valid-witness strategy makes universality equivalent to nonacceptance.
- One omitted valid history is enough to make the grammar's language non-universal.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
21. Q: Why does the ALLCFG construction use a PDA before converting it into a context-free grammar?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A PDA is easier to design for nondeterministically guessing and checking a reason that a string is an invalid history.

The target language contains every string except valid accepting histories. A PDA can nondeterministically choose one of several defects to verify: an incorrect beginning, a nonaccepting ending, or an illegal transition between adjacent configurations. For the transition case, it guesses a configuration, pushes it onto the stack, and compares the next configuration while popping. A standard construction converts the resulting PDA into an equivalent CFG. This approach separates the conceptual recognition strategy from the grammar's production-rule bookkeeping. The correctness argument must show that every invalid history is accepted along some branch and no valid accepting history is accepted by any branch.

Study instruction:
- "Describe the PDA's three nondeterministic branches and explain why nondeterministic guessing is appropriate for recognizing a union of error types."

Key point:
- The PDA recognizes the union of detectable error cases.
- An equivalent CFG can be obtained by the standard PDA-to-CFG construction.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
22. Q: Why are alternate configurations reversed in the ALLCFG computation-history encoding?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Reversing every other configuration lets a stack compare a stored configuration with the next one in the required order.

A PDA pushes the symbols of one configuration onto its stack and later pops them in reverse order. If consecutive configurations were both written in the same left-to-right orientation, the popped sequence would be reversed relative to the next configuration, making direct position-by-position comparison awkward. The construction therefore writes every other configuration backward. When the PDA pushes one orientation and pops it, the resulting order aligns with the adjacent configuration's representation. The alternating convention is a technical encoding device; it does not change the simulated machine's transition relation. The verifier must account for the orientation when checking head movement and local updates.

Study instruction:
- "Take a short configuration string, reverse it, and simulate the push/pop order to see how the next comparison becomes aligned."

Key point:
- The alternating reversal is introduced to accommodate LIFO stack order.
- The representation must preserve enough positional information to verify each transition.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
23. Q: What is the Post Correspondence Problem (PCP)?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: PCP asks whether a finite collection of string-pair dominos can be arranged in a nonempty sequence, with repetition allowed, so the concatenated top and bottom strings are identical.

An instance consists of finitely many pairs (t1,b1),…,(tk,bk), often pictured as dominos with a top string and a bottom string. A solution is an index sequence i1,…,iℓ with ℓ≥1 such that ti1ti2…tiℓ = bi1bi2…biℓ. An index may occur more than once. The problem is to determine whether any such sequence exists. The order of selected dominos matters, as does repetition; the sequence is not required to use every domino. PCP is a decision problem about a finite description, but the search space of possible sequences is unbounded.

Study instruction:
- "For a small PCP instance, write the top and bottom concatenations separately for a proposed index sequence and compare them character by character."

Key point:
- A PCP match is a finite nonempty sequence of indices.
- The same domino may be used repeatedly, and unused dominos are permitted.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
24. Q: What makes a sequence of dominos a match in PCP?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: The concatenation of all selected top strings must equal the concatenation of all selected bottom strings exactly.

If the chosen indices are i1,…,iℓ, the top result is ti1ti2…tiℓ and the bottom result is bi1bi2…biℓ. A match exists only if these two complete strings are identical in length and in every symbol position. Individual dominos do not need to have equal top and bottom strings or equal lengths. A partial sequence may have one side temporarily ahead of the other; only the completed concatenations must match. This is why a useful manual strategy is to track the unmatched suffix of the longer concatenation rather than insisting that each domino align by itself.

Study instruction:
- "When testing a candidate sequence, maintain the unmatched suffix after each added domino and reject only when the already-produced prefixes disagree."

Key point:
- Equality is required for the full concatenations, not for each individual pair.
- A partial length imbalance is not automatically a contradiction.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
25. Q: Why is PCP undecidable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: An instance can be constructed so that a match exists exactly when a given Turing machine accepts its input.

The proof reduces ATM to PCP. Starting with M and w, construct a domino system whose matches encode an accepting computation history of M on w. The dominoes are designed so that the top and bottom strings can stay aligned only when each next configuration is a legal successor of the preceding one. A complete match can be closed only after the simulated machine reaches its accept state. If M accepts w, the accepting history yields a match. If M does not accept w, no sequence can complete the required alignment. Therefore a PCP decider would decide ATM, which is impossible. The proof uses a modified version of PCP first and then removes its extra first-domino restriction.

Study instruction:
- "Summarize the correspondence between a PCP match and an accepting history, including how the construction prevents an invalid transition from matching."

Key point:
- The reduction encodes a computation, not merely a collection of arbitrary string coincidences.
- A PCP decider would decide ATM through the effective instance construction.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
26. Q: What is the Modified Post Correspondence Problem (MPCP), and how does it differ from PCP?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: MPCP requires the match to begin with a designated first domino; ordinary PCP has no such requirement.

In PCP, a solution may begin with any domino in the instance. In MPCP, the first selected domino is prescribed, and subsequent dominos may be chosen from the available collection with repetition as usual. The restriction is useful for reductions because the first domino can force the bottom string to begin with the initial configuration of a Turing machine computation. The chapter first constructs an MPCP instance simulating M on w, then transforms that instance into an ordinary PCP instance by adding marker symbols that make the designated first domino the only possible starting choice for a match.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the definitions and explain why forcing the first domino simplifies the encoding of the initial configuration."

Key point:
- MPCP adds a starting-index constraint to PCP.
- The constraint is later encoded into the domino strings themselves.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
27. Q: How do PCP dominoes simulate a Turing machine transition?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Special dominoes encode the local change around the head, while copy dominoes preserve tape symbols unaffected by the transition.

A Turing machine configuration is represented as a string containing tape symbols and the current state marker at the head position. If δ(q,a)=(r,b,R), a transition domino replaces the local pattern qa with br, representing writing b and moving the state marker right. For a left move, the domino must also include the symbol immediately to the left of the head so that the new state marker appears in the correct position. Copy dominoes reproduce tape symbols away from the head. A delimiter domino carries the configuration boundary forward, and an additional blank-symbol domino can extend the represented tape to the right when needed. The construction forces each layer of the match to describe a legal successor configuration.

Study instruction:
- "Choose one right-moving and one left-moving transition and write the local top and bottom fragments that encode each update."

Key point:
- Transition dominoes update only the region affected by the head move.
- Copy and delimiter dominoes preserve the rest of the configuration structure.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
28. Q: Why does the PCP simulation need special handling for the accepting state?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: The match must be able to close only after the simulated machine has accepted.

The transition dominoes simulate ordinary computation steps, but a completed PCP match also needs a controlled way to make the top and bottom strings catch up. The construction adds special dominoes for the accept state that consume adjacent tape symbols in a sequence of cleanup steps. These are pseudo-steps used only to erase the remaining configuration mismatch in the string alignment; they are not actual transitions of the original Turing machine. A final domino closes the delimiters and completes equality. Because this cleanup mechanism is available only when the accept-state marker appears, a match cannot finish merely because the machine reaches a rejecting state or continues forever.

Study instruction:
- "Separate the real simulated transitions from the artificial cleanup phase and identify the symbol that enables the latter."

Key point:
- The cleanup phase is a device for finishing string equality after acceptance.
- The construction must not provide a corresponding completion route for rejection.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
29. Q: How is the first-domino requirement of MPCP eliminated to obtain PCP?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Interleaving a fresh marker symbol with the strings makes the transformed first domino the only possible start of a match.

For a string u=u1u2…un, define marker-interleaved forms by inserting a new symbol such as * before or after each original symbol. Transform each MPCP domino so that the marker pattern preserves the original alignment while ensuring that only the transformed first domino has matching initial symbols on its top and bottom. Add a special ending domino that supplies the final marker needed to equalize the two concatenations. Any PCP match must therefore start with the transformed first domino and finish using the designated end device. Removing the markers from a transformed match recovers an MPCP match, and every MPCP match yields a PCP match under the transformation.

Study instruction:
- "Prove both directions: a PCP match in the transformed instance gives an MPCP match, and an MPCP match can be expanded into a PCP match."

Key point:
- The marker symbols enforce a structural condition that PCP does not state explicitly.
- A valid reduction must preserve solutions in both directions.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
30. Q: Why does the undecidability of MPCP together with MPCP ≤m PCP imply that PCP is undecidable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Undecidability transfers from the source to the target along a valid mapping reduction.

The chapter constructs a reduction from ATM to MPCP and then a computable transformation from MPCP instances to PCP instances. If MPCP is undecidable and MPCP ≤m PCP, a PCP decider would solve MPCP by first transforming each MPCP instance and then running the PCP decider. That contradicts MPCP's undecidability. More generally, if A ≤m B and A is undecidable, then B is undecidable. The order of the reductions matters: the target of the first reduction must be the source of the next, and the composed construction must preserve yes- and no-instances.

Study instruction:
- "Draw the chain ATM ≤m MPCP ≤m PCP and explain what a hypothetical PCP solver would imply at each stage."

Key point:
- Undecidability propagates forward along a reduction chain.
- Every transformation in the chain must be computable and correctness-preserving.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
31. Q: What is a computable function in the context of mapping reducibility?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A function is computable if a Turing machine halts on every input with the function value written as output.

A computable function f:Σ→Σ is realized by a Turing machine that, on every input w, eventually halts with exactly f(w) on its tape. Totality matters: a procedure that may run forever on some inputs is not a computable function under this definition. Ordinary arithmetic operations on encoded integers are examples, as are effective transformations of machine descriptions. For instance, a machine description can be transformed by adding states that implement a specified behavior. In a reduction, the transformation must be computable independently of the answer to the source problem; it cannot secretly solve the undecidable question while constructing the target instance.

Study instruction:
- "For a proposed reduction function, specify how its output is constructed and why every syntactically valid input produces an output after finitely many steps."

Key point:
- A mapping reduction must be an effective total transformation.
- Constructing a machine description is allowed even when running that machine would not terminate.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
32. Q: What is mapping reducibility, and how is it written formally?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A is mapping reducible to B, written A ≤m B, if a computable function f satisfies w ∈ A iff f(w) ∈ B for every string w.

Mapping reducibility, also called many-one reducibility, requires a single computable transformation f from strings to strings. The defining equivalence is w∈A if and only if f(w)∈B for every w in the domain Σ*. Thus f maps yes-instances of A to yes-instances of B and no-instances of A to no-instances of B. The equivalence is stronger than merely preserving positive instances. Once f is available, membership in A can be tested by computing f(w) and querying membership in B. The formal definition therefore captures the informal idea that a solver for B can be used to solve A through a uniform preprocessing step.

Study instruction:
- "Memorize the biconditional and test it on one yes-instance and one no-instance of a simple proposed reduction."

Key point:
- The reduction function must be computable.
- Both positive and negative answers must be preserved.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
33. Q: Why must a mapping reduction preserve both membership and nonmembership?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A decider for B returns yes or no, so the transformed instance must give the correct answer for either status of the original input.

The condition w∈A iff f(w)∈B has two directions. If w is in A, f(w) must be in B; if w is not in A, f(w) must not be in B. Preserving only yes-instances would not prevent a no-instance from being mapped to a yes-instance, causing a target decider to give a false positive. Likewise, preserving only no-instances could cause false negatives. The biconditional ensures that the target solver's answer can be copied directly to solve the source problem. In some informal reductions the answer is deliberately inverted, but a mapping reduction must encode that inversion into the construction so that the formal membership equivalence still holds.

Study instruction:
- "For a candidate f, build a two-row truth table for w∈A and f(w)∈B. Reject the reduction if either row violates the biconditional."

Key point:
- A reduction is a many-one equivalence of membership status.
- Answer reversal must be explicit in the construction, not left implicit.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
34. Q: How does the theorem A ≤m B and B decidable imply that A is decidable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Compute the reduction f(w), then run the decider for B on the result.

Let f be a computable reduction from A to B and let M be a decider for B. Construct N that, on input w, computes f(w), runs M on f(w), and returns M's answer. The computation of f halts on every w by definition. M also halts on every input because it is a decider. The equivalence w∈A iff f(w)∈B guarantees that N accepts exactly the strings in A and rejects all others. Therefore N is a decider for A. This theorem is the fundamental direction check behind reduction arguments: decidability of the target transfers backward to the source.

Study instruction:
- "Write the composite decider N and justify termination separately for the reduction computation and the target decider."

Key point:
- A ≤m B means B decidable implies A decidable.
- The target decider is used only after the computable transformation finishes.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
35. Q: Why does A ≤m B and A undecidable imply that B is undecidable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: If B were decidable, the preceding theorem would make A decidable, contradicting the premise.

Assume A≤mB and suppose, for contradiction, that B is decidable. The theorem on mapping reducibility then gives a decider for A: compute the reduction and invoke the B decider. But A is known to be undecidable. The contradiction shows that B cannot be decidable. Notice the direction: the known hard problem must reduce to the target problem. Showing B≤mA does not establish B's undecidability from A's, because a solver for A would then solve B, which is consistent with B being easier.

Study instruction:
- "Before using the corollary, write down which language is already known to be undecidable and verify that its reduction arrow points to the target."

Key point:
- Known undecidable source → target proves target undecidable.
- Reversing the arrow invalidates this inference.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
36. Q: How can ATM be mapping reduced to HALTTM by transforming the machine description?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Construct M′ that accepts when M accepts and loops when M rejects, then map ⟨M,w⟩ to ⟨M′,w⟩.

Define M′ to simulate M on its input x. If M accepts, M′ accepts; if M rejects, M′ enters an infinite loop. If M itself loops, M′ also loops. The mapping function takes ⟨M,w⟩ and outputs ⟨M′,w⟩. If M accepts w, M′ halts on w. If M does not accept w, M′ does not halt on w, whether because M rejects or loops. Thus ⟨M,w⟩∈ATM exactly when ⟨M′,w⟩∈HALTTM. The transformation only edits a finite machine description and is computable. Improperly encoded inputs can be mapped to a fixed string outside the target language.

Study instruction:
- "Check all three behaviors of M on w and verify that only acceptance becomes halting in M′."

Key point:
- The constructed machine converts acceptance into halting.
- A reduction can encode a semantic condition by changing the control flow of a machine.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
37. Q: Why is mapping reducibility transitive?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Two computable reductions can be composed to produce one computable reduction.

Suppose A≤mB via computable f and B≤mC via computable g. Define h(x)=g(f(x)). To compute h, first run a machine computing f on x, then run a machine computing g on the resulting string. Both halt on every input, so their sequential composition also halts on every input. Moreover, x∈A iff f(x)∈B, and f(x)∈B iff g(f(x))∈C. Therefore x∈A iff h(x)∈C, which proves A≤mC. Transitivity allows chains of reductions, such as ATM≤mMPCP≤mPCP, to be combined into a single reduction.

Study instruction:
- "Prove computability and membership preservation as two separate parts of the transitivity argument."

Key point:
- The composite reduction is g∘f, not f∘g.
- The direction of each intermediate equivalence must align.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
38. Q: What is the mapping reduction from ETM to EQTM?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Map a machine M to the pair consisting of M and a fixed machine that recognizes the empty language.

Let M∅ be a machine that rejects every input. Define f(⟨M⟩)=⟨M,M∅⟩. This function is computable because it copies the encoding of M and appends a fixed encoding of M∅. The pair belongs to EQTM exactly when L(M)=L(M∅)=∅, which is exactly the condition that ⟨M⟩ belongs to ETM. Thus ETM≤mEQTM. The reduction illustrates a general technique: embed a single-object property into a two-object comparison by fixing one side to a known reference object.

Study instruction:
- "State the language recognized by the fixed machine and substitute it directly into the definition of EQTM."

Key point:
- The fixed comparison machine must recognize exactly the empty language.
- The reduction compares recognized languages, not the internal transition tables.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
39. Q: Why does the chapter distinguish an informal reduction from a mapping reduction?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: An informal solver-based construction may reverse answers or use a different logical relationship, while mapping reducibility requires a computable function preserving membership exactly.

An informal reduction is often presented by assuming a target solver and describing how to use it to answer a source question. This is enough for some undecidability contradictions, but the source answer may be obtained by reversing the target solver's answer. Mapping reducibility fixes a precise form: w∈A iff f(w)∈B. For example, the emptiness construction from ATM produces a machine whose language is empty when M does not accept w, so the straightforward function maps ATM to the complement of ETM rather than to ETM itself. Since complementation preserves decidability, the informal contradiction still proves ETM undecidable, but it does not establish the claimed mapping reduction ATM≤mETM.

Study instruction:
- "For each informal proof, write the exact truth condition relating the source instance to the constructed target instance before assigning a ≤m symbol."

Key point:
- Not every solver-based reduction is automatically a mapping reduction in the desired direction.
- Answer inversion changes which language is the exact mapping target.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
40. Q: Why is ATM not mapping reducible to ETM in the example discussed in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Such a reduction would conflict with the recognizability properties of ATM and ETM.

If ATM≤mETM, then because ETM is Turing-recognizable, the recognizability-preservation theorem would imply that ATM is Turing-recognizable. But ATM is not Turing-recognizable. Therefore no such mapping reduction exists. The informal proof that ETM is undecidable does not contradict this fact: its construction maps acceptance to nonemptiness, equivalently maps ATM to the complement of ETM. Decidability is closed under complement, so this is enough for an undecidability contradiction, but it is not the same as a mapping reduction from ATM to ETM.

Study instruction:
- "Use the recognizability theorem as a test for whether a proposed reduction can exist, and distinguish ETM from its complement."

Key point:
- A valid informal undecidability argument need not yield the particular mapping reduction one first expects.
- Recognizability transfer can prove that a proposed mapping reduction is impossible.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
41. Q: How does mapping reducibility preserve Turing-recognizability?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: If A ≤m B and B has a recognizer, then A has a recognizer that computes the reduction before running the B recognizer.

Let f reduce A to B and let R recognize B. On input w, construct a recognizer S that computes f(w) and then simulates R on f(w). If w∈A, f(w)∈B, so R eventually accepts and S accepts. If w is not in A, f(w) is not in B, so R does not accept; it may reject or loop, and S is allowed to behave the same way. The reduction computation always halts, so it does not interfere with the recognizer's permitted nontermination on negative inputs. Therefore A is Turing-recognizable whenever B is.

Study instruction:
- "Compare this proof with the decider theorem and identify exactly where the proof allows nontermination."

Key point:
- Recognizers must accept members but may loop on nonmembers.
- The computable reduction still must halt on every input.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
42. Q: What follows if A ≤m B and A is not Turing-recognizable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: B cannot be Turing-recognizable.

The contrapositive of recognizability preservation states that if A≤mB and A is not Turing-recognizable, then B is not Turing-recognizable. If B had a recognizer, composing it with the reduction would recognize A, contrary to the premise. This is a standard way to prove that a target problem is beyond recognition, which is stronger than merely proving it undecidable. For example, the complement of ATM is not Turing-recognizable, so reducing that language to a target B establishes that B is not Turing-recognizable.

Study instruction:
- "Identify a known non-recognizable source language before attempting to prove that a new language is not recognizable."

Key point:
- Non-recognizability also transfers forward from source to target.
- Undecidability alone does not imply non-recognizability.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
43. Q: How does the chapter show that EQTM is neither Turing-recognizable nor co-Turing-recognizable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: It gives one reduction from the complement of ATM to EQTM and another from the complement of ATM to the complement of EQTM.

To show EQTM is not Turing-recognizable, reduce the complement of ATM to EQTM. Given ⟨M,w⟩, compare a machine M1 that always rejects with M2 that simulates M on w and accepts every input if that simulation accepts. The pair is equivalent exactly when M does not accept w. To show the complement of EQTM is not Turing-recognizable, use a machine M1 that always accepts instead; the pair is inequivalent exactly when M does not accept w. Since the complement of ATM is not recognizable, recognizability transfer rules out recognizers for both target languages. Therefore EQTM is neither recognizable nor co-recognizable.

Study instruction:
- "Construct both fixed comparison machines and write the condition under which the pair is equivalent in each construction."

Key point:
- Changing the fixed machine from always reject to always accept flips the equivalence condition.
- A language is co-Turing-recognizable when its complement is Turing-recognizable.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
44. Q: What is the difference between decidability, Turing-recognizability, and co-Turing-recognizability?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A decider halts on every input; a recognizer must accept members but may loop on nonmembers; a co-recognizer recognizes the complement.

A language is decidable if some Turing machine halts on every input and accepts exactly its members. It is Turing-recognizable if a machine accepts every member but may either reject or loop on a nonmember. It is co-Turing-recognizable if its complement is Turing-recognizable. A language is decidable exactly when it and its complement are both Turing-recognizable. These distinctions matter in reductions: mapping reducibility transfers recognizability backward from B to A, and non-recognizability forward from A to B. It does not allow one to infer recognizability merely from undecidability or from the fact that the complement is undecidable.

Study instruction:
- "Make a three-column comparison of the halting requirements for deciders, recognizers, and co-recognizers, then add an example of a negative instance for each."

Key point:
- Every decidable language is recognizable and co-recognizable.
- Some undecidable languages are recognizable; ATM is a standard example.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
45. Q: How should malformed encodings be handled in a mapping reduction?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Define the reduction on every string, usually sending malformed source encodings to a fixed string outside the target language.

The formal definition requires a computable function on all strings, not just well-formed descriptions. A reduction machine should first check whether its input has the required syntactic form. If it is malformed, it can output a fixed nonmember of B, provided that this choice preserves the intended equivalence for malformed strings, which are nonmembers of the encoded source language. For valid encodings, it performs the intended machine transformation. The fixed output must itself be a known nonmember and its encoding must be computable. Omitting malformed inputs leaves a gap in the proof of totality and in the biconditional required by mapping reducibility.

Study instruction:
- "Choose a fixed target nonmember and show that every malformed source string is mapped to it while valid strings follow the main construction."

Key point:
- A reduction must be defined for all strings in its domain.
- The malformed-input branch is part of the computability and correctness proof.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
46. Q: How can one design a reduction when the target problem asks whether a property exists?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Build a target object whose witnesses correspond exactly to successful computations of the source instance.

First identify what a positive answer to the source problem means. If it means that M accepts w, create an object whose accepted witnesses are encodings of accepting computations, or whose structural property can hold only after such acceptance. Then prove that a source yes-instance yields at least one target witness and a source no-instance yields none. The LBA emptiness reduction is an example: the LBA recognizes accepting histories, so its language is nonempty exactly when M accepts w. The PCP construction similarly uses a matching sequence as a witness for a valid accepting computation. The construction should also address malformed candidate witnesses so that they cannot create false positives.

Study instruction:
- "Write the intended witness in one sentence before defining the target object; then design checks that make every accepted witness valid."

Key point:
- Existence reductions encode a source yes-instance as a target witness.
- Soundness requires ruling out spurious witnesses.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
47. Q: What is Rice's theorem, and how does it relate to the chapter's undecidability results?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Rice's theorem states that every nontrivial property of the language recognized by a Turing machine is undecidable.

A property is a language property when it depends only on L(M), not on the particular machine description or implementation. It is nontrivial if it holds for at least one Turing-machine language and fails for at least one other. Rice's theorem says that deciding any such nontrivial semantic property of L(M) is undecidable. Examples include whether L(M) is empty, finite, infinite, regular, or equal to Σ*. The chapter's ETM and REGULARTM results are representative instances of this phenomenon. Rice's theorem does not apply to purely syntactic properties of a machine description, such as whether a particular state name occurs in the encoding, because equivalent machines may differ syntactically.

Study instruction:
- "For each proposed property, ask whether two machines recognizing the same language must receive the same answer and whether the property is nontrivial."

Key point:
- Rice's theorem concerns semantic properties of recognized languages.
- Nontriviality and dependence only on L(M) are both essential conditions.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
48. Q: Why does Rice's theorem not automatically decide properties of a Turing machine's internal syntax?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: The theorem applies only when the property is invariant under language equivalence.

A syntactic property may change when a machine is rewritten without changing its recognized language. For example, one machine may contain an unreachable state while an equivalent machine has that state removed. Whether a state is named q7 or whether a transition is written in a particular format is not determined by the recognized language. Such properties fall outside Rice's theorem's hypotheses. They may still be undecidable, but that requires a separate proof, often by a tailored reduction. Conversely, a simple syntactic property may be decidable by inspecting the finite description. The key is not whether a property sounds complicated, but whether it depends only on the language and is nontrivial.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a semantic property such as language emptiness with a syntactic property such as the number of states, and test invariance under equivalent machine descriptions."

Key point:
- Language invariance is a required hypothesis of Rice's theorem.
- Failure of Rice's hypotheses does not prove decidability or undecidability by itself.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
49. Q: What are the most common direction errors when applying mapping reductions?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: The main error is reversing the reduction arrow or drawing a conclusion from the wrong side of the implication.

If A≤mB, then a decider or recognizer for B can be used to decide or recognize A. Therefore, known undecidability or non-recognizability of A can be transferred to B. The reverse conclusions do not follow: B being undecidable says nothing by itself about A, and A being decidable says nothing by itself about B. A useful check is to imagine that a solver for B has been handed to you and ask whether the reduction genuinely lets you answer A. If not, the arrow is likely reversed or the construction is incomplete. Also distinguish a reduction to B from a reduction to the complement of B, since answer inversion changes the exact conclusion.

Study instruction:
- "For every reduction exercise, write the solver-transfer sentence before attempting the proof. If it does not read naturally from B back to A, revisit the direction."

Key point:
- The reduction arrow points from source A to target B, but solver power flows from B back to A.
- Complementing the target must be accounted for explicitly.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
50. Q: How can one verify that a proposed reduction construction is computable?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Show that it manipulates finite encodings by a terminating mechanical procedure and never waits for the undecidable event being encoded.

A reduction may add states, hard-code an input string, concatenate finite strings, insert marker symbols, or combine fixed machine descriptions. These are effective operations on finite representations. The construction must not simulate M on w until it accepts before producing the output, because that could run forever and would make the transformation partial. Instead, it builds a new machine whose future behavior depends on that simulation. The reduction function itself then halts after writing the new description, even if the constructed machine later loops. This distinction between constructing a program and executing it is central to many proofs in the chapter.

Study instruction:
- "Underline every operation in the construction and classify it as finite string manipulation or execution of the encoded computation. Ensure only the former is performed by the reduction function."

Key point:
- The reduction outputs a description; it need not run the described machine.
- A computable reduction cannot depend on knowing whether the source computation eventually accepts.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
51. Q: What is the role of a fixed reference machine in reductions between language properties?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: It provides a known language against which the target problem can test a property of the input machine.

A fixed reference machine can simplify a general semantic question into a comparison question. For ETM≤mEQTM, the reference machine recognizes ∅, so equivalence with it means that the other machine also recognizes ∅. For other reductions, a machine recognizing Σ* or a carefully chosen regular or nonregular language may serve as the reference. The reference object must have exactly the intended language, and the reduction must construct its encoding uniformly. This technique is especially useful when the target problem compares two machines, grammars, or other formal objects.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the exact language of the reference machine and write the comparison predicate in terms of that language before defining the reduction."

Key point:
- A reference object converts a unary property into a binary comparison.
- The language recognized by the reference object is what matters.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
52. Q: How does the chapter distinguish the acceptance problem for an LBA from the emptiness problem for an LBA?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: ALBA asks about one fixed input and is decidable; ELBA asks whether any input is accepted and is undecidable.

For ALBA, the input contains both an LBA M and a string w. Because M is confined to n tape cells on that input, it has only finitely many configurations, so a bounded simulation decides acceptance. ELBA receives only M and asks whether there exists some string w that M accepts. The input length is no longer fixed, so the finite configuration bound varies with each possible witness and cannot be used to test all strings exhaustively. The computation-history reduction proves that ELBA is undecidable by making its accepted strings correspond to accepting histories of an unrestricted Turing machine.

Study instruction:
- "Write ALBA and ELBA using quantifiers over strings, then explain why a bound for one input does not produce a bound over all possible inputs."

Key point:
- A finite configuration space for each fixed input does not make the language-emptiness question decidable.
- The quantifier over all possible input strings changes the problem.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
53. Q: Why is the PCP match sequence allowed to repeat indices, and why does this matter for the reduction?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Repeated indices let the sequence represent an arbitrarily long computation even though the domino set is finite.

A Turing machine may perform an unbounded number of transitions before accepting, so a finite domino set must be reusable to encode histories of arbitrary finite length. Allowing repeated indices permits a transition domino, copy domino, or delimiter domino to appear repeatedly as the simulated computation advances through successive configurations. The finite instance describes the available local operations; the solution sequence supplies as many uses as the particular accepting history requires. Prohibiting repetition would sharply change the problem and would not support the same simulation argument.

Study instruction:
- "Relate the number of dominoes in the instance to the number of domino selections in a match; they need not be equal."

Key point:
- The PCP instance is finite, but a match may be arbitrarily long.
- Repetition supports repeated use of the same transition patterns.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
54. Q: How should the proof of an undecidability reduction separate soundness from completeness?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Completeness shows every source yes-instance creates a target yes-instance; soundness shows no source no-instance can create one.

For a reduction from A to B, completeness is the implication w∈A ⇒ f(w)∈B. Soundness is the converse f(w)∈B ⇒ w∈A. In the computation-history construction, completeness shows that if M accepts w, its actual accepting history is accepted by the constructed verifier or yields a PCP match. Soundness shows that any accepted history or match must encode a legal accepting computation of M on w. Without soundness, an invalid sequence could produce a false positive; without completeness, a real accepting computation might fail to yield a target witness. The two directions together establish the required biconditional.

Study instruction:
- "Write two separate paragraphs titled only in your working notes “yes-instance preservation” and “no-instance preservation,” then combine them into the formal proof."

Key point:
- A reduction is correct only when both directions hold.
- Soundness rules out spurious target solutions; completeness guarantees genuine source solutions are represented.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
55. Q: What practical checklist can be used to evaluate a new mapping-reduction proof?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Check the source and target definitions, total computability, both directions of the membership equivalence, and the solver-transfer consequence.

Begin by writing the exact languages A and B and confirming which one is already known to be hard. Define f on every input string, including malformed encodings. Explain why f is computable without solving the source instance. Prove w∈A iff f(w)∈B, separately checking yes- and no-cases. Verify that the target object is well formed and that any fixed machines or grammars have the claimed languages. Finally, state the theorem that transfers decidability, recognizability, or non-recognizability in the required direction. If the construction reverses an answer, identify whether the exact target is B or its complement.

Study instruction:
- "Apply this checklist to the ATM-to-HALTTM construction and the ETM-to-EQTM construction without referring to the proof text."

Key point:
- A reduction proof is a correctness proof for a computable transformation.
- The final undecidability conclusion is only as strong as the verified membership equivalence.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
56. Q: How do the chapter's examples illustrate different styles of undecidability reductions?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: They use direct machine modification, comparison with a fixed object, finite computation histories, and string-alignment gadgets.

HALTTM is handled by using a halting test to make simulation safe. ETM restricts a constructed machine so that only one designated input can witness nonemptiness. REGULARTM switches between a regular and a nonregular language. EQTM fixes one comparison machine to recognize the empty language. ELBA and ALLCFG encode accepting computation histories and ask about emptiness or universality. PCP turns transitions into domino alignments. These constructions share the same logical goal but adapt the encoding to the target problem's vocabulary. Selecting a reduction is therefore a design task: choose a target property that can be made to reflect the source computation.

Study instruction:
- "For each target problem, identify the gadget used and the exact source behavior that makes the gadget's property true."

Key point:
- The reduction method is general, but its construction is tailored to the target language.
- A useful source problem is one whose behavior can be encoded naturally in the target structure.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
57. Q: What is the significance of a repeated configuration in a deterministic computation?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Once a deterministic machine repeats a configuration, its future computation repeats forever.

A configuration contains all information needed to determine the next step: state, tape contents, and head position. If a deterministic machine reaches the same configuration twice, the transition function prescribes the same successor each time. Therefore the subsequent sequence of configurations repeats, producing an infinite cycle. This observation underlies the ALBA decider: once the number of possible configurations is exhausted without halting, a repetition must have occurred. The argument would need adjustment for nondeterministic machines, because a repeated configuration may have different future branches; the chapter's simple bounded simulation proof is stated for deterministic LBAs.

Study instruction:
- "Explain why equality of the entire configuration matters; a repeated state alone is not enough to conclude that the computation will repeat."

Key point:
- A deterministic configuration fully determines the next configuration.
- Repeating only the control state does not prove a loop because tape contents or head position may differ.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
58. Q: Why can a computation-history verifier use local checks to validate an entire computation?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: A history is valid exactly when its initial configuration is correct, every adjacent pair is a legal transition, and its final configuration has the required status.

The global claim that a long sequence is a valid computation can be decomposed into local conditions. The first configuration is checked against the known start configuration. For each adjacent pair, the verifier checks that one transition of M transforms the earlier configuration into the later one. The last configuration is checked for acceptance. If every local condition holds, induction over the sequence shows that each configuration is the one reached after the corresponding number of steps from the start. Therefore the final accepting configuration is genuinely reachable. This local-to-global reasoning is why computation histories can be encoded as strings and checked by restricted automata.

Study instruction:
- "State the induction invariant for the ith configuration: it is exactly the configuration reached after i−1 legal steps from the start."

Key point:
- Local transition validity plus a correct start determines the whole run inductively.
- The final-state test supplies the acceptance condition.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Undecidable Problems and Post Correspondence Problem
59. Q: What limitations should be remembered when interpreting the chapter's undecidability results?
A: Show answer
Answer overview: Undecidability rules out a total algorithm for every input; it does not rule out useful partial procedures, restricted cases, or practical approximations.

A proof that a language is undecidable establishes that no Turing machine halts on every input while correctly deciding membership for all encoded instances. It does not say that no instance can ever be solved, that no recognizer exists, or that all practical cases are difficult. Some undecidable problems have recognizable yes-instances; some restricted variants are decidable, as ALBA demonstrates. A proof also depends on the exact formal definition of the problem: changing the input model, alphabet, restrictions, or required output can change decidability. Practical tools may still solve many instances, but they cannot be guaranteed correct and terminating for every possible input if the formal problem is undecidable.

Study instruction:
- "For each theorem, record the exact problem definition and distinguish the universal impossibility claim from what remains possible for restricted or typical instances."

Key point:
- Undecidability is a worst-case impossibility over all valid instances.
- A restricted version of an undecidable problem may be decidable.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 9 — Reducibility
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Lecture 10 — Computation History Method
- Cengage: Introduction to the Theory of Computation, Third Edition
